Truncated smooth function spaces Thanks: This research was partially supported by French National Research Agency (ANR-10-LABX-0070), (ANR-11-IDEX-0007) (O.D.) and by PID2020-114948GB-I00, 2017 SGR 358, AP 14870758, the CERCA Programme of the Generalitat de Catalunya, and Severo Ochoa and María de Maeztu Program for Centers and Units of Excellence in R & \& D (CEX2020-001084-M) (S.T.).
Oscar Domínguez, Sergey Tikhonov

Abstract
. We introduce truncated Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin function spaces and investigate their main properties: embeddings, interpolation, duality, lifting, traces. These new scales allow us to improve several known results in functional analysis and PDE’s.



2010 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 46E35, 42B35; Secondary 26A15, 46E30, 46B70, 65T60

1.
Introduction

Smooth function spaces are basic notion in analysis. The main examples are
Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces, since those scales contain various distinguished spaces of distributions such as standard Sobolev, Gagliardo–Slobodeckiĭ, fractional Sobolev, Bessel potential spaces, Hölder–Zygmund spaces, spaces of BMO-type, Hardy spaces, and Lebesgue spaces. Another important scale is Lipschitz spaces, in particular containing the space of bounded variation.
All mentioned scales have shown to be crucial in functional and harmonic analysis and PDE’s, among other fields.
Key properties of these spaces, such as duality, interpolation, embeddings, characterizations, traces, and lifting, have been extensively developed since the middle of the last century. See e.g. the monographs
[
BS88
,
BL76
,
BIN78
,
N75
,
Pe76
,
ST87
,
Ste70
,
Tri78
,
Tri83
,
Tri92
,
Tri01
]
.

We also stress that in recent times a big deal of attention has been devoted to function spaces with generalized smoothness. Indeed, in many questions arising in different areas it has become apparent that the scope of classical smoothness is rather limited and thus the finer tuning given by generalized smoothness becomes very useful. Here we mention pointwise multipliers
[
LSYY22
]
, elliptic PDE’s and geometric measure theory
[
MMPT22
]
, probability theory and stochastic processes
[
FL06
,
KSV22
]
, sharp embedding theorems
[
Ha07
]
, fractal analysis and a related spectral theory
[
Tri97
]
, capacity theory
[
LWXY21
]
, regularity properties of Euler solutions
[
OT00
,
CCW11
]
, regularity of flows for the transport and continuity equation
[
CDeL08
,
Le18
,
BN20
]
, or optimal recovery
[
HKNV22
]
. The prototype of function spaces with generalized smoothness is the logarithmic Besov spaces
B^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces
F^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, which can be defined through Fourier-analytical decompositions as follows:

(1.1)

\|f\|_{B^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jsq}(1+j)^{bq}\|(\varphi_{j}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\bigg)^{\frac{1}{q}}

and

(1.2)

\|f\|_{F^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jsq}(1+j)^{bq}|(\varphi_{j}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

In the special case
b=0
one recovers the classical spaces
B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, respectively.
Interestingly, the most unexpected effects can be discovered dealing with zero smoothness, that is,
s=0.
In particular, the space
B^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
does not coincide with its counterpart
{\bf{B}}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
defined by differences. In fact, the lack of adequate Littlewood–Paley decompositions makes the analysis of
\mathbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
rather tricky; we refer the reader to
[
DT18
]
for a detailed study of
\mathbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, including some discussions on applications of these spaces to different areas of analysis (see
[
DT18
, Section 1.2]
).

The main goal of this work is to introduce the
truncated scale
of smooth function spaces. Let us illustrate this idea by presenting truncated Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces:

(1.3)

\|f\|_{T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jbr}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}2^{\nu sq}\|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\right)^{1/r},

(1.4)

\|f\|_{T^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jbr}\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}2^{\nu sq}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{r}\right)^{1/r}.

Roughly speaking, the additional summability parameter
r
enables to refine classical norms via truncations with respect to dyadic decomposition.

There are several aspects of the matter worth pointing out, which show that truncated spaces are extremely important in the theory of function spaces, PDE’s, interpolation theory, potential theory and applications. Next we highlight some of them.

1.
A unified approach to classical spaces and applications to PDE’s.

As one may expect, choosing appropriately the smoothness and summability parameters
b
and
r
, respectively, in
T^{b}_{r}X
we recover the prototypical space
X
. For example,

T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=B^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
q=r
and, in particular,
T^{0}_{q}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.
More than that, specifying parameters in the definitions of truncated Besov and Triebel-Lizorkin spaces, one can recover in a unifying fashion
all classical scales of smooth function spaces: Besov
B^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, Lipschitz
\text{Lip}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, Triebel–Lizorkin
F^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, Besov spaces with zero smoothness
{{B}}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
{\textbf{B}}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In particular, we obtain

T^{b+1/q}_{q}F^{0}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})={\textbf{B}}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\qquad b>-1/q,

and

T^{b+1/q}_{q}F^{s}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\text{Lip}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\qquad b<-1/q;

cf. Proposition
3.8
.

Another motivation to introduce truncated smooth function spaces comes from applications to PDE’s. In his fundamental paper
[
Vi99
]
, Vishik was able to extend the classical Yudovich theory for uniqueness of non-Lipschitz
2
D Euler flows via Besov-type
B_{\Gamma}
spaces defined in terms of growth properties of partial sums involving
L_{\infty}
-norms of dyadic frequencies:

\|f\|_{B_{\Gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\sup_{j\geq 0}\,\frac{1}{\Gamma(j)}\,\sum_{\nu=0}^{j}\|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

where
\Gamma
is a positive and increasing function such that
\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\Gamma(j)}=\infty
. Thus the main result of
[
Vi99
, Theorem 7.1]
guarantees uniqueness of the Euler flow for related vorticities that are bounded in
B_{\Gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, with
\Gamma(j)=j
. In particular, for this special choice of
\Gamma
, the embeddings

bmo(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow B^{0}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow B_{\Gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

leads to uniqueness of Euler flows for
bmo(\mathbb{R}^{d})
vorticities, extending thus the classical
L_{\infty}
-based theory due to Yudovich
[
Yu63
,
Yu95
]
.

Observe that
B_{\Gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\,\Gamma(j)=j^{\alpha},\,\alpha\leq 1,
is a special case of truncated Besov spaces, namely,

B_{\Gamma}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=T^{-\alpha}_{\infty}B^{0}_{\infty,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

see Proposition
3.5
. Accordingly, truncated constructions (
1.3
) and (
1.4
) arise naturally in the analysis of PDE’s.

2.
Embeddings.
Truncated smooth function spaces allow us to sharpen classical embeddings. To clarify this, we mention the well-known embedding between Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin (in particular, Sobolev) spaces

B^{s}_{p,\min\{p,q\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow B^{s}_{p,\max\{p,q\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

which is not sharp anymore in the scale of truncated spaces. Namely, we have (cf. Corollary
3.11
)

B^{s}_{p,\min\{p,q\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow T_{\min\{p,q\}}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow T_{\max\{p,q\}}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow B^{s}_{p,\max\{p,q\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

and any of these embeddings are sharp. Here,
T_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=T^{0}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

As already mentioned above, the scales of
\mathbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\text{Lip}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
spaces are not contained in the scales of Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces (cf. (
1.1
)-(
1.2
)).
The known embeddings between them involve log-shifts of smoothness:

(1.5)

B^{0,b+1/\min\{2,p,q\}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow\textbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow B^{0,b+1/\max\{2,p,q\}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

and

(1.6)

B^{s,b+1/\min\{2,p,q\}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow\text{Lip}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow B^{s,b+1/\max\{2,p,q\}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Furthermore, these embeddings are optimal within the scale of classical Besov spaces; cf.
[
DHT20
]
and
[
DT18
]
. As a byproduct of general embedding theorems between truncated spaces, we are now able to show that both (
1.5
) and (
1.6
) can be considerably sharpened by dealing with the refined scale of truncated spaces. Namely, we establish (cf. Corollaries
11.9
and
11.10
)

T^{b+1/q}_{q}B^{0}_{p,\min\{2,p\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow\mathbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow T^{b+1/q}_{q}B^{0}_{p,\max\{2,p\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

and

T^{b+1/q}_{q}B^{s}_{p,\min\{2,p\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow\text{Lip}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow T^{b+1/q}_{q}B^{s}_{p,\max\{2,p\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Furthermore, they are optimal.

3.
Interpolation.

One of the main properties of Besov spaces is that they are closed under real interpolation. A more complete result states that, for
A,\widetilde{A}\in\{B,F\}
,

(1.7)

(A^{s_{0}}_{p,q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\widetilde{A}^{s_{1}}_{p,q_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{\theta,r}=B^{s}_{p,r}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

where
s=(1-\theta)s_{0}+\theta s_{1}
and
s_{0}\neq s_{1}
.
It is a natural question to understand whenever this property holds for truncated spaces.
We positively answer this question (cf. Theorem
4.17
):

(T^{b_{0}}_{r_{0}}A^{s_{0}}_{p,q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),T^{b_{1}}_{r_{1}}\widetilde{A}^{s_{1}}_{p,q_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{\theta,r}=B^{s,b}_{p,r}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\qquad s_{0}\neq s_{1}.

Moreover, in the borderline case
s_{0}=s_{1}=s
, we obtain that (cf. Theorems
4.7
and
4.14
)

(T^{b_{0}}_{r_{0}}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),T^{b_{1}}_{r_{1}}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{\theta,r}=T^{b}_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\qquad b_{0}\neq b_{1}.

Another intrinsic question can be stated as follows. Can one obtain the truncated spaces as a result of interpolation between the classical ones?
An answer is again positive and rather surprising since modern interpolation methods are naturally appearing:
limiting interpolation
. In particular, we have
for
s_{0}>s
and
b>0

(1.8)

T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=(B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),A^{s_{0}}_{p,q_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{(0,b-1/r),r},

and for
s_{0}<s
and
b<0

(1.9)

T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=(A^{s_{0}}_{p,q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{(1,b-1/r),r};

see Theorem
4.2
. A similar result holds for truncated Triebel–Lizorkin spaces (cf. Theorem
4.8
). As special cases, we can characterize zero-smoothness Besov and Lipschitz spaces via limiting interpolation:

\mathbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=(L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),A^{s_{0}}_{p,q_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{(0,b),q},\qquad b>-1/q,

\text{Lip}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=(A^{s_{0}}_{p,q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),H^{s}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{(1,b),q},\qquad b<-1/q.

The formulae (
1.8
)-(
1.9
) will play a key role in our arguments, since they put truncated function spaces into the powerful framework provided by limiting interpolation. In particular, this enables to transfer many properties of classical spaces to truncated spaces.

4.
Lifting properties.
Consider the lifting operator

I_{\sigma}=(\text{id}-\Delta)^{\frac{\sigma}{2}},\qquad\sigma\in\mathbb{R}.

In particular, if
\sigma<0
then
I_{\sigma}
is the classical
Bessel potential
. A crucial property of Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces is their stability under liftings. More precisely, for
A\in\{B,F\}
,
I_{\sigma}
acts as an isomorphism from
A^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
onto
A^{s-\sigma,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

\|I_{\sigma}f\|_{A^{s-\sigma,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\asymp\|f\|_{A^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

On the other hand, the mapping properties of liftings on Besov spaces of zero smoothness and Lipschitz spaces are much more delicate and not completely understood. For instance, a partial answer asserts that

I_{\sigma}:\mathbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to B^{-\sigma,b+1/\max\{2,p,q\}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

and

I_{\sigma}:B^{\sigma,b+1/\min\{2,p,q\}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\to\mathbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Furthermore, the shifts given by
\frac{1}{\max\{2,p,q\}}
and
\frac{1}{\min\{2,p,q\}}
are optimal. In particular, this result tells us that classical Besov spaces are not sharp enough to characterize lifting properties of
\mathbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. This drawback can be now overcome with the help of truncated spaces.

In Section
8.2
, we show that
I_{\sigma}
acts as an isomorphism from
\mathbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
onto
T^{b+1/q}_{q}F^{-\sigma}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and

\|I_{\sigma}f\|_{T^{b+1/q}_{q}F^{-\sigma}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\asymp\|f\|_{\mathbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Recall that
\mathbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=T^{b+1/q}_{q}F^{0}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Accordingly, lifting properties of
\mathbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
should be understood within the more general setting provided by truncated spaces. A similar statement also holds true for
\text{Lip}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

5.
Duality.

In Section
9
, we obtain that the scale of truncated spaces is closed under duality, extending then the well-known duality assertions related to classical Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces. Specifically (cf. Theorem
9.1
for precise statement) we have

(T^{b}_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{\prime}=T^{-b}_{r^{\prime}}A^{-s}_{p^{\prime},q^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\qquad A\in\{B,F\}.

In particular, this yields a complete answer to the open question of characterizing the dual spaces of
\mathbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\text{Lip}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
:

(\mathbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{\prime}=T^{-b-1/q}_{q^{\prime}}F^{0}_{p^{\prime},2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\qquad(T^{-b-1/q}_{q^{\prime}}F^{0}_{p^{\prime},2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{\prime}=\mathbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

and

(\text{Lip}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{\prime}=T^{-b-1/q}_{q^{\prime}}F^{-s}_{p^{\prime},2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\qquad(T^{-b-1/q}_{q^{\prime}}F^{-s}_{p^{\prime},2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))^{\prime}=\text{Lip}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

6.
Applications.
We would like to mention here at least three new applications of truncated spaces in different areas.

(i) The trace operator can be defined (at least formally) by

(\text{Tr }f)(x)=(\text{Tr }_{\{x_{d}=0\}}f)(x)=f(x^{\prime},0),\qquad x=(x^{\prime},x_{d}),\quad x^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1},\qquad d\geq 2.

The well-known trace theorem for Besov spaces states that

\text{Tr }:B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\longrightarrow B^{s-1/p}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})

for
p\geq 1,\,s>1/p
, and
0<q\leq\infty
.
Furthermore,
Tr
admits a right inverse
Ex
, which is a
bounded linear operator from
B^{s-1/p}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})
into

B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. See e.g.
[
Tri83
, Section 2.7.2]
.

In the limiting case
p=1
and/or
s=1/p
, we mention the classical results by Gagliardo
[
Ga57
]
:

(1.10)

\text{Tr}:\text{BV}(\mathbb{R}^{d+1}_{+})\to L_{1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

and by Peetre
[
Pe75
]
, Agmon–Hörmander
[
AH76
]
, and Frazier–Jawerth
[
FJ85
]
:

(1.11)

\text{Tr }:B^{1/p}_{p,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\longrightarrow L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})

and there exist bounded extension operators. Importantly, there does not exist a bounded linear extension related to (
1.10
) or (
1.11
), which yields serious obstructions; cf.
[
Pe79
]
(see also
[
Tri83
]
for further historical comments).

Motivated by the above discussion, very recently Malý, Shanmugalingam and Snipes
[
MSS18
]
considered the Besov space with zero smoothness
1
1

1

The corresponding norm appears frequently in the literature under the name of
Dini-type condition
.

\mathbf{B}^{0}_{1,1}=\mathbf{B}^{0,0}_{1,1}
, which is a slightly smaller space than
L_{1}
. It turns out that in this case a bounded linear extension operator does exist:

(1.12)

\text{Ex}:\mathbf{B}^{0}_{1,1}(\partial\Omega)\to\text{BV}(\Omega)

such that
\text{Tr}\circ\text{Ex}=\text{id}
. Here
\Omega
is a smooth bounded domain in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Working with truncated spaces allows us to give a full solution to the trace/extension problem in the limiting case
s=1/p
(cf. (
1.10
) and (
1.11
)). In particular, we can substantially improve the above mentioned result by Malý, Shanmugalingam and Snipes as follows (cf. Theorem
17.1
)

\text{Tr }:T^{b+1/q}_{q}B^{1/p}_{p,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\longrightarrow\mathbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})

and there exists a linear extension operator
Ex
which is
continuous from
\mathbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})

into
T^{b+1/q}_{q}B^{1/p}_{p,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with

\text{Tr }\circ\text{Ex }=\text{id}.
Specializing this result with
b=0
and
p=q=1
, we obtain

\text{Tr }:B^{1,1}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\longrightarrow\mathbf{B}^{0}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})

(cf. (
1.10
)-(
1.11
)) and there exists a bounded linear extension operator

\text{Ex}:\mathbf{B}^{0}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d-1})\to B^{1,1}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

with
\text{Tr }\circ\text{Ex }=\text{id}.
Note that
B^{1,1}_{1,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subsetneq\text{BV}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(see (
1.12
)).

(ii) An important embedding for elliptic and parabolic equations is the prominent result by Trudinger
[
Tru67
]
:

W^{d/p}_{p}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\hookrightarrow L_{\infty}(\log L)_{-1/p^{\prime}}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),\qquad p\in(1,\infty).

Later on this result was sligtly improved with respect to both domain and target spaces:

B^{d/p}_{p,q}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\hookrightarrow L_{\infty,q}(\log L)_{-1}(\mathbb{T}^{d}).

In its turn, this embedding can be naturally considered in the more general setting of Besov spaces of logarithmic smoothness. Namely, if
b<-1/q
then

(1.13)

B^{d/p,b+1/\min\{1,q\}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\hookrightarrow L_{\infty,q}(\log L)_{b}(\mathbb{T}^{d}).

The last embedding has been the object of intensive research since 1979 (see the paper
[
DRS79
]
) until now (see
[
CaMou04b
,
CaFar06
,
CaLeo06
,
Mar08
,
MNP08
]
). It is known that
(
1.13
) is optimal within the scale of Besov spaces of logarithmic smoothness.
However, we will show that one can sharpen (
1.13
) with the help of the truncated Besov spaces
T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
.
Namely, we show (cf. Theorem
17.10
)

T^{b+1/q}_{q}B^{d/p}_{p,1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})\hookrightarrow L_{\infty,q}(\log L)_{b}(\mathbb{T}^{d})

and this embedding is optimal (cf. Remark
17.11
).

The counterpart of (
1.13
) with
b>-1/q
is formulated in terms of the zero-smoothness Besov space
\mathbf{B}_{\infty,q}^{0,b}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. More precisely, if
b>-1/q
then

(1.14)

B^{d/p,b+1/\min\{1,q\}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow\mathbf{B}_{\infty,q}^{0,b}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

This embedding is a crux of matter in function spaces. It served as a basis for the theory of continuity envelopes developed in detail by Haroske–Triebel
[
Tri01
,
Ha07
]
and it has been further investigated in
[
MNP09
,
MNS11
,
MNS14
]
and the references within. Similarly as (
1.13
), the embedding (
1.14
) is optimal within the scale of classical Besov spaces,
but we can now prove that it admits the following improvements within the scale of truncated spaces (cf. Theorem
17.13
)

T^{b+1/q}_{q}B^{d/p}_{p,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow\mathbf{B}^{0,b}_{\infty,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

and this is optimal.

(iii) The celebrated Bourgain–Brezis–Mironescu formula
[
BBM01
]
states that

\lim_{s\to 1-}(1-s)^{1/p}\|f\|_{\dot{W}^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=c_{d,p}\,\|\nabla f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

In some sense it fixes the well-known defect of the fractional Sobolev (Gagliardo–Slobodeckiĭ) seminorms

\|f\|_{\dot{W}^{s,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\ \bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|^{p}}{|x-y|^{d+sp}}\,dx\,dy\bigg)^{1/p},\quad s\in(0,1),

that they do not converge to the standard Sobolev seminorm
\|\nabla f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
as
s\to 1-
.
The explicit value of the constant
c_{d,p}
is known, depending only on
d
and
p
.
This result has been widely considered recently
with respect to
different perspectives: interpolation theory
[
Mil05
]
, higher-order Besov spaces
[
KMX05
]
, Triebel–Lizorkin spaces
[
BSY21
]
, anisotropic Sobolev spaces
[
Lu14
]
, etc.

We will show that the family of truncated norms on Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces satisfies the Bourgain–Brezis–Mironescu phenomenon, in the sense that under a certain normalization, one can attain the classical norms
\|\cdot\|_{A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\,A\in\{B,F\}
, via limits.
Specifically, one has (cf. Theorem
17.20
)

\lim_{b\to 0-}(1-2^{br})^{1/r}\,\|f\|^{*}_{T^{b}_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\|f\|_{A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

To close this introduction, we would like to note that truncated Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces introduced in this paper can be understood within the abstract setting provided by interpolation theory.
In the forthcoming work
[
DT22
]
, we propose a novel interpolation method,
the truncated interpolation
, which contains as distinguished examples truncated Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces. It is worth mentioning that this methodology goes further beyond the setting of smooth function spaces and it enables us to define many other truncated scales
T^{b}_{r}X
with the special choice of a space
X
.

Notation.

Given two non-negative quantities
A
and
B
, we use
A\lesssim B
for the estimate
A\leq CB
, where
C
is a positive constant which is independent of all essential parameters in
A
and
B
. If
A\lesssim B\lesssim A
, then we write
A\asymp B
.

Let
X
and
Y
be quasi-Banach spaces. We write
X\hookrightarrow Y
if the identity operator from
X
into
Y
is continuous, that is,
\|f\|_{Y}\lesssim\|f\|_{X}
for
f\in X
. By
X=Y
we mean that
X\hookrightarrow Y\hookrightarrow X
, i.e.,
\|f\|_{X}\asymp\|f\|_{Y}
. We use
X^{\prime}
to denote the dual space of
X
and
p^{\prime}
is the dual exponent of
p\in(0,\infty)
, i.e.,
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{\prime}}=1
if
p\geq 1
and
p^{\prime}=\infty
if
p\leq 1
.

As usual,
\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes the
d
-dimensional Euclidean space,
\mathbb{R}=\mathbb{R}^{1}
,
\mathbb{C}
is the set of all complex numbers,
\mathbb{T}^{d}
is the
d
-dimensional torus,
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{T}^{1}
,
\mathbb{N}
is the collection of all natural numbers,
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
,
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
is the
d
-dimensional integer lattice,
\mathbb{Z}=\mathbb{Z}^{1}
. For some
a\in\mathbb{R}
, we let
a_{+}=\max\{a,0\}.

Given a quasi-Banach space
X
and
p\in(0,\infty)
, the Bochner space
L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};X)
is formed by all strongly measurable functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to X
such that

\|f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d};X)}=\bigg(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\|f(\xi)\|_{X}^{p}\,d\xi\bigg)^{1/p}<\infty.

In the special case
X=\mathbb{R}
or
X=\mathbb{C}
, we simply write
L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the classical Lebesgue space.

Let
s\in\mathbb{R},q\in(0,\infty]
and let
X
be a quasi-Banach space. Then
\ell^{s}_{q}(X)
is the sequence space formed by all those
x=(x_{j})\subset X
such that

\|x\|_{\ell^{s}_{q}(X)}=\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}[2^{js}\|x_{j}\|_{X}]^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}<\infty

(with the usual modification if
q=\infty
). In the special case
X=\mathbb{C}
(or
X=\mathbb{R}
), we simply write
\ell^{s}_{q}
. In addition, if
s=0
then we recover
\ell_{q}
.

2.
A quick review on classical function spaces: Besov, Triebel–Lizorkin and Lipschitz

We collect here basic definitions and standard notations in the theory of function spaces. By
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
we denote the Schwartz space of all complex-valued, infinitely differentiable and rapidly decreasing functions on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and by
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
the dual space of all tempered distributions in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
then

(2.1)

\widehat{\varphi}(\xi)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-ix\cdot\xi}\varphi(x)\,dx,\qquad\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

denotes the Fourier transform of
\varphi
. The symbol
\varphi^{\vee}
stands for the inverse Fourier transform and is given by the right-hand side of (
2.1
) with
i
in place of
-i
. Both the Fourier transform and the inverse Fourier transform are extended to
\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
in the usual way. Let
(\varphi_{j})_{j=0}^{\infty}
be the usual dyadic resolution of unity in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Let
p,q\in(0,\infty]
and
s,b\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
B^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the
Besov space
formed by all
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

(2.2)

\|f\|_{B^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jsq}(1+j)^{bq}\|(\varphi_{j}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}<\infty

(with the usual convention if
q=\infty
). The
Triebel–Lizorkin space

F^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\,p<\infty,
is defined as the collection of all
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

(2.3)

\|f\|_{F^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jsq}(1+j)^{bq}|(\varphi_{j}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\infty

(with the usual modification made if
q=\infty
). Standard references for the theory of these spaces are
[
KL87
]
,
[
Mou01
]
and
[
FL06
]
; see also
[
DT18
]
for a recent account. In particular, in the absence of logarithmic smoothness (i.e.,
b=0
) one recovers classical Besov spaces,
B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces,
F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, studied systematically in the monographs
[
N75
,
Pe76
,
Tri83
,
Tri01
]
.

For
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
p\in(1,\infty)
, the
(fractional) Sobolev space

H^{s}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(also known as
Bessel-potential space
) is formed by all
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

\|f\|_{H^{s}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\|((1+|\xi|^{2})^{s/2}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}<\infty.

Recall that
H^{s}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=F^{s}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(with equivalence of norms) and, in particular,
W^{k}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=F^{k}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\,k\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
, the classical
Sobolev space
formed by all
f\in L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
whose weak derivatives
D^{l}f,\,|l|\leq k
, belong to
L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and equipped with the norm

\|f\|_{W^{k}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\sum_{|l|\leq k}\||D^{l}f|\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

It is well known that Besov–Triebel–Lizorkin spaces can be equivalently introduced in terms of differences, see e.g.
[
Tri83
, 2.5.12]
,
[
KL87
]
and
[
HaMou04
]
. For
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
f\in L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\,1\leq p\leq\infty
, the
k
-th order modulus of smoothness

\omega_{k}(f,t)_{p}
is defined by

(2.4)

\omega_{k}(f,t)_{p}=\sup_{|h|\leq t}\|\Delta^{k}_{h}f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\qquad t>0,

where
\Delta^{k}_{h}
is the
k
-th difference with step
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
given by

(\Delta^{1}_{h}f)(x)=(\Delta_{h}f)(x)=f(x+h)-f(x),\qquad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

and
\Delta_{h}^{k+1}=\Delta_{h}^{k}\Delta_{h}
. It is clear that

(2.5)

\Delta^{k}_{h}f(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{k}(-1)^{j}{k\choose j}f(x+(k-j)h).

Let
1\leq p\leq\infty,0<q\leq\infty,0\leq s<k
and
b\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
\mathbf{B}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the set of all
f\in L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
having finite quasi-norm

(2.6)

\|f\|_{\mathbf{B}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\|f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\bigg(\int_{0}^{1}(t^{-s}(1-\log t)^{b}\omega_{k}(f,t)_{p})^{q}\frac{dt}{t}\bigg)^{1/q}

(with the usual modification if
q=\infty
). It is well known that

(2.7)

\|f\|_{\mathbf{B}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\asymp\|f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\bigg(\int_{|h|<1}|h|^{-sq-d}(1-\log|h|)^{bq}\|\Delta^{k}_{h}f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}dh\bigg)^{1/q}

and different values of
k
with
k>s
in the right-hand sides of (
2.6
) and (
2.7
) give equivalent quasi-norms on
\mathbf{B}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We also mention that these spaces admit extensions to the case
p\in(0,1)
. For the special choice
b=0
, we deal with classical spaces
\mathbf{B}^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

The limiting parameter
s=0
in
\mathbf{B}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
deserves special attention. In this case, we may assume
b\geq-1/q\,(b>0\text{ if }q=\infty)
. The latter condition on
b
is not restrictive since
\mathbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
b<-1/q
. In particular, the elements in
\mathbf{B}^{0}_{p,1}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
are defined in terms of the validity of the classical
L_{p}
-Dini condition

(2.8)

\int_{0}^{1}\omega_{k}(f,t)_{p}\frac{dt}{t}<\infty.

This condition is widely used in functional analysis, probability theory, harmonic analysis and PDE’s. The logarithmic refinement of (
2.8
) provided by (
2.6
) (with
s=0
) is also important in applications. In this regard we only refer to the survey contained in
[
DT18
, Section 1.2]
.

As already mentioned above, a classical result in the theory of function spaces asserts that

(2.9)

B^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\mathbf{B}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\qquad s\in(0,k),\qquad p\in[1,\infty].

However, the limiting case
s=0
in (
2.9
) fails to be true.
Similarly, putting

s=k
in (
2.6
) we do not recover

B^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In fact,
the expression given in the right-hand side of (
2.6
) with
s=k
corresponds to a special case of Lipschitz space. More precisely, for
1\leq p\leq\infty,0<q\leq\infty,s>0
and
b<-1/q\,(b\leq 0\text{ if }q=\infty)
, the space
\text{Lip}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is formed by all those
f\in L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

\int_{0}^{1}t^{-sq}(1-\log t)^{bq}\omega_{s}(f,t)_{p}^{q}\frac{dt}{t}<\infty

(with the usual change if
q=\infty
). Here
\omega_{s}(f,t)_{p}
denotes the classical (if
s\in\mathbb{N}
) or fractional modulus of smoothness, which can be naturally introduced after replacing the classical differences
\Delta^{k}_{h}
(cf. (
2.5
)) in (
2.4
) by

\Delta^{s}_{h}f(x)=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(-1)^{j}{s\choose j}f(x+(s-j)h)

with
{s\choose j}=\frac{s(s-1)\ldots(s-j+1)}{j!}
and
{s\choose 0}=1
, more precisely,

\omega_{s}(f,t)_{p}=\sup_{|h|\leq t}\|\Delta^{s}_{h}f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

The space
\text{Lip}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is endowed with the quasi-norm

\|f\|_{\text{Lip}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\|f\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}+\bigg(\int_{0}^{1}t^{-sq}(1-\log t)^{bq}\omega_{s}(f,t)_{p}^{q}\frac{dt}{t}\bigg)^{1/q}.

The assumption
b<-1/q
is natural, otherwise the space
\text{Lip}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
becomes trivial (in the sense that it is formed only by the zero element). These spaces play a key role in embedding theorems and PDE’s and they were intensively investigated in
[
EH99
,
EH00
,
Ha07
]
(and the references quoted there) if
s=1
and in
[
DHT20
]
for general
s>0
. In particular, a bounded function
f
belongs to
\text{Lip}^{1,b}_{\infty,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
if and only if

|f(x+h)-f(h)|\lesssim|h|(1-\log|h|)^{-b}\qquad\text{for all}\qquad|h|<1.

In the special case
b=0
one recovers the classical Lipschitz condition. On the other hand,

(2.10)

\text{Lip}^{s,0}_{p,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=H^{s}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

As already mentioned above

\textbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\neq B^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\text{Lip}^{k,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\neq B^{k,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

(cf. (
2.9
)). However, it was recently shown in
[
DHT20
, Theorem 4.1]
and
[
DT18
, Section 9.1]
that it is still possible to establish relations between these three scales of function spaces. Namely, let
p\in(1,\infty)
and
q\in(0,\infty]
then

(2.11)

B^{0,b+1/\min\{2,p,q\}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow\textbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow B^{0,b+1/\max\{2,p,q\}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\qquad\text{if}\qquad b>-1/q,

and, if
s>0
then

(2.12)

B^{s,b+1/\min\{2,p,q\}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow\text{Lip}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow B^{s,b+1/\max\{2,p,q\}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\qquad\text{if}\qquad b<-1/q.

Furthermore, these embeddings are optimal within the class of Besov spaces. We mention that versions of (
2.11
) and (
2.12
) in the limiting cases
p=1,\infty
are also available in
[
DHT20
,
DT18
]
.

3.
Truncated Besov and Triebel–Lizorkin spaces: definitions and basic properties

The main objects that we study in this paper are new spaces of smooth functions, which are obtained via truncation methods of the classical quasi-norms (
2.2
) and (
2.3
) with
b=0
. In particular, these spaces contain as distinguished examples the Besov spaces
B^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the Triebel–Lizorkin spaces
F^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, the Besov spaces of smoothness near zero
\mathbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and the Lipschitz spaces
\text{Lip}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Definition 3.1
.

(i)

Let
p,q,r\in(0,\infty]
and
s,b\in\mathbb{R}
. The truncated Besov
space
T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is formed by all
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for which

\|f\|_{T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jbr}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}2^{\nu sq}\|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\right)^{1/r}

is finite (with the usual convention if
q=\infty
and/or
r=\infty
).

(ii)

Let
p\in(0,\infty),q,r\in(0,\infty]
and
s,b\in\mathbb{R}
. The truncated Triebel–Lizorkin space
T^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is formed by all
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for which

\|f\|_{T^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jbr}\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}2^{\nu sq}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{r}\right)^{1/r}

is finite (with the usual convention if
q=\infty
and/or
r=\infty
).

(iii)

Let
p\in(0,\infty),q,r\in(0,\infty]
and
s,b\in\mathbb{R}
. The inner truncated Triebel–Lizorkin space
\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is formed by all
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for which

\|f\|_{\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jbr}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}2^{\nu sq}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\bigg)^{1/r}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

is finite (with the usual convention if
q=\infty
and/or
r=\infty
).

Notation 3.2
.

For
A\in\{B,F\}
, we let
T_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):=T^{0}_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\mathfrak{T}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}):=\mathfrak{T}^{0}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

Remark 3.3
.

(i)

Let
A\in\{B,F\}
. It is plain to see that
T^{b}_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
are quasi-Banach spaces (Banach space if
p,q,r\geq 1
).

(ii)

One can also introduce the counterparts of the spaces
T^{b}_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
related to general smooth resolutions of unity in the sense of
[
Tri83
, Section 2.3.1, page 45]
. However the definition of these spaces do not depend (up to equivalence of quasi-norms) on the choice of the resolution of unity. The proof of this fact is a simple consequence of multiplier assertions (cf.
[
Tri83
, Section 2.3.2, page 46]
for further details).

(iii)

The periodic counterparts
T^{b}_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{T}^{d}),\,A\in\{B,F\},
and
\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
can be introduced in the same fashion.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
A\in\{B,F\},p,q,r\in(0,\infty]\,(p<\infty\text{ if }A=F)
and
s,b\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

(3.1)

\|f\|_{T^{b}_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\asymp\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jbr}\bigg\|\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\bigg\|^{r}_{A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\bigg)^{1/r}.

Proof.

Set
\varphi_{-1}\equiv 0
and
\widetilde{\varphi}_{l}:=\varphi_{l-1}+\varphi_{l}+\varphi_{l+1}
for
l\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
,. By basic Fourier multiplier assertions (cf.
[
Tri83
, Theorem 1.5.2]
),

\displaystyle\bigg\|\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\bigg\|_{B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}2^{lsq}\bigg\|\bigg(\varphi_{l}\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f}\bigg)^{\vee}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=2^{j}-2}^{2^{j+1}-1}2^{lsq}\bigg\|\bigg(\varphi_{l}\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f}\bigg)^{\vee}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{l=2^{j}-2}^{2^{j+1}-1}2^{lsq}\|(\varphi_{l}\widetilde{\varphi}_{l}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=2^{j}-2}^{2^{j+1}-1}2^{lsq}\|(\varphi_{l}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}.

On the other hand, for
j\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\sum_{l=2^{j}-2}^{2^{j+1}-1}2^{lsq}\bigg\|\bigg(\varphi_{l}\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f}\bigg)^{\vee}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{l=2^{j}}^{2^{j+1}-3}2^{lsq}\bigg\|\bigg(\varphi_{l}\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f}\bigg)^{\vee}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=2^{j}}^{2^{j+1}-3}2^{lsq}\|(\varphi_{l}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}.

This gives (
3.1
) with
A=B
. The corresponding assertion with
A=F
follows similar ideas as above but now relying on vector-valued multiplier theorems, as can be found e.g. in
[
Tri83
, Theorem 1.6.3]

∎

We provide some elementary equivalent quasi-norms on truncated function spaces.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
p,q,r\in(0,\infty],s\in\mathbb{R}
and
b\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\{0\}
.

(i)

Assume
b>0
. Then

\|f\|_{T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\asymp\|f\|^{*}_{T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jbr}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{\infty}2^{\nu sq}\|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\right)^{1/r},

and if additionally
p<\infty
then

\|f\|_{T^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\asymp\|f\|^{*}_{T^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jbr}\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{\infty}2^{\nu sq}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{r}\right)^{1/r},

\|f\|_{\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\asymp\|f\|^{*}_{\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jbr}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{\infty}2^{\nu sq}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\bigg)^{1/r}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

(ii)

Assume
b<0
. Then

\|f\|_{T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\asymp\|f\|^{*}_{T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jbr}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=0}^{2^{j}}2^{\nu sq}\|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\right)^{1/r},

and if additionally
p<\infty
then

\|f\|_{T^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\asymp\|f\|^{*}_{T^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jbr}\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{\nu=0}^{2^{j}}2^{\nu sq}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{r}\right)^{1/r},

\|f\|_{\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\asymp\|f\|^{*}_{\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jbr}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=0}^{2^{j}}2^{\nu sq}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\bigg)^{1/r}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Remark 3.6
.

We stress that the definitions of
\|\cdot\|_{T^{b}_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{*}
for
A\in\{B,F\}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
depend on the sign of
b
.

Proof of Proposition
3.5
.

We will only prove

\|f\|_{T^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\asymp\|f\|^{*}_{T^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

The corresponding assertions for
T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
follow similar ideas.

Obviously
\|f\|_{T^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\|f\|^{*}_{T^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. To show the converse estimate, we can argue as follows. Let
b>0
, by triangle inequality and Hardy’s inequality (
B.4
),

\displaystyle\|f\|^{*}_{T^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jbr}\bigg\|\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{\infty}2^{\nu sq}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q}\bigg\|_{L_{p/q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{r/q}\right)^{1/r}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jbr}\bigg(\sum_{l=j}^{\infty}\bigg\|\sum_{\nu=2^{l}-1}^{2^{l+1}-2}2^{\nu sq}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q}\bigg\|^{\min\{1,p/q\}}_{L_{p/q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\bigg)^{r/(q\min\{1,p/q\})}\right)^{1/r}

\displaystyle\asymp\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jbr}\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}2^{\nu sq}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}\bigg\|^{r}_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right)^{1/r}=\|f\|_{T^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

The case
b<0
can be obtained similarly but now invoking (
B.3
).
∎

Remark 3.7
.

The case
b=0
in
T^{b}_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\,A\in\{B,F\},
and
\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
needs special care. This assertion can be justified by the following two facts. Firstly, Proposition
3.5
provides two natural candidates for quasi-norms on
T^{0}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, namely

(3.2)

\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{\infty}2^{\nu sq}\|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\right)^{1/r}

and

(3.3)

\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=0}^{2^{j}}2^{\nu sq}\|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\right)^{1/r}.

However (
3.3
) is not a meaningful condition since it is only satisfied by zero function:

(3.4)

\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=0}^{2^{j}}2^{\nu sq}\|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}<\infty\iff f\equiv 0.

Indeed, the trivial estimate

\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=0}^{2^{j}}2^{\nu sq}\|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\geq\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\|(\varphi_{0}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

holds.
On the other hand, condition (
3.2
) does make sense and then
T^{*}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is formed by all
f\in\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

(3.5)

\|f\|_{T^{*}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{\infty}2^{\nu sq}\|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\right)^{1/r}<\infty.

In particular, in the special case
r=q
we have

(3.6)

\|f\|_{T^{*}_{q}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\asymp\|f\|_{B^{s,0,1/q}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}[2^{js}(1+\log(1+j))^{1/q}\|(\varphi_{j}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}]^{q}\right)^{1/q},

a
Besov space of iterated logarithmic smoothness
(cf.
[
DT18
, p. 32]
). Analogously, the space
T^{*}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is defined by

(3.7)

\|f\|_{T^{*}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}:=\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{\infty}2^{\nu sq}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{r}\right)^{1/r}<\infty.

Clearly

(3.8)

T^{*}_{\infty}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\qquad A\in\{B,F\}.

Secondly

T^{*}_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\neq T_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\qquad A\in\{B,F\}.

Indeed, to fix some ideas consider first the special case
r=q
and
A=B
, by above considerations (cf. (
3.6
)) we have
T^{*}_{q}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=B^{s,0,1/q}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, but on the other hand it is clear that
T_{q}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(we will see in Proposition
3.8
below further relationships between truncated and classical function spaces). Note that
B^{s,0,1/q}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subsetneq B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In fact, this particular case illustrates what happens in the general setting. More precisely, it is clear that
T^{*}_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow T_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. However, this embedding is strict. To see this one may consider lacunary Fourier series of the form

(3.9)

f(x)\sim\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}a_{j}e^{i2^{j}x_{1}}\psi(x),\qquad x=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

where
\psi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\backslash\{0\}
with
\text{supp }\psi\subset\mathbb{T}^{d}
and
(a_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}}
is a scalar-valued sequence. It is plain to check that

(3.10)

(\varphi_{j}\widehat{f})^{\vee}(x)=a_{j}e^{i(2^{j}-2)x_{1}}\psi(x),\qquad j\geq 0.

In particular, if
a_{j}=2^{-js}j^{-1/q}(1+\log(1+j))^{-\varepsilon}
where
\max\{1/r,1/q\}<\varepsilon<1/r+1/q
, then

\displaystyle\|f\|_{T^{*}_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\asymp\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}}^{\infty}(1+\log\nu)^{-\varepsilon q}\frac{1}{\nu}\bigg)^{r/q}\right)^{1/r}

\displaystyle\asymp\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{-\varepsilon r+r/q}\bigg)^{1/r}=\infty

but

\|f\|_{T_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\asymp\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}j^{-\varepsilon r}\right)^{1/r}<\infty.

Hence
f\in T_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\backslash T^{*}_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

3.1.
Relationships between truncated and classical spaces

The following result provides a table of coincidences between the scale of spaces
T^{b}_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\,A\in\{B,F\},\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, introduced in Definition
3.1
and the classical spaces considered in Section
2
.

Proposition 3.8
.

(i)

Let
p,q\in(0,\infty]
and
s,b\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

T^{b}_{q}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=B^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(ii)

Let
p\in(0,\infty),q\in(0,\infty]
and
s,b\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{q}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=F^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(iii)

Let
p\in(1,\infty),q\in(0,\infty]
and
b>-1/q
. Then

T^{b+1/q}_{q}F^{0}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\emph{\textbf{B}}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(iv)

Let
p\in(1,\infty),q\in(0,\infty],s>0
and
b<-1/q
. Then

T^{b+1/q}_{q}F^{s}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\emph{Lip}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Proof.

(i): We have

\|f\|_{T^{b}_{q}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\asymp\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}2^{\nu sq}(1+\nu)^{bq}\|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\right)^{1/q}=\|f\|_{B^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

The item (ii) can be obtained in a similar fashion as (i).

(iii): It was shown in
[
CDT16
, Theorem 4.3]
that the space
\textbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
can be equivalently defined through the Fourier transform, namely,

(3.11)

\|f\|_{\textbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\asymp\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(1+j)^{bq}\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{\nu=j}^{\infty}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\right)^{1/q}.

Therefore the proof will be completed if we show that the right-hand side of the previous expression is equivalent to
\|f\|_{T^{b+1/q}_{q}F^{0}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. To proceed with, we first note that, by (
3.11
) and monotonicity properties,

(3.12)

\|f\|_{\textbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\asymp\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{j(b+1/q)q}\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{\infty}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\right)^{1/q}

and thus
\|f\|_{T^{b+1/q}_{q}F^{0}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim\|f\|_{\textbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
. To prove the converse estimate, according to (
3.12
), the fact that
\ell_{1}\hookrightarrow\ell_{2}
and the triangle inequality, we obtain

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\textbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\asymp\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{j(b+1/q)q}\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{l=j}^{\infty}\sum_{\nu=2^{l}-1}^{2^{l+1}-2}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\right)^{1/q}

(3.13)

\displaystyle\leq\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{j(b+1/q)q}\bigg(\sum_{l=j}^{\infty}\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{l}-1}^{2^{l+1}-2}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\bigg)^{q}\right)^{1/q}.

If
q\leq 1
then, using (
3.13
), the embedding
\ell_{q}\hookrightarrow\ell_{1}
and changing the order of summation, we get

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\textbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{j(b+1/q)q}\sum_{l=j}^{\infty}\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{l}-1}^{2^{l+1}-2}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\right)^{1/q}

\displaystyle\asymp\bigg(\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}2^{l(b+1/q)q}\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{l}-1}^{2^{l+1}-2}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}=\|f\|_{T^{b+1/q}_{q}F^{0}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

On the other hand, if
q>1
we can apply Hardy’s inequality in (
3.13
) (since
b+1/q>0
) resulting in

\|f\|_{\textbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{j(b+1/q)q}\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{2}\bigg)^{1/2}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\right)^{1/q}=\|f\|_{T^{b+1/q}_{q}F^{0}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

(iv): See Theorem
A.1
in Appendix A.

∎

We continue with some elementary relations for
T^{b}_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
for
A\in\{B,F\}
and
\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. In particular, we show the important property that embeddings between classical spaces
A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
are preserved under the action of truncation
T^{b}_{r}
. To be more precise, we establish the following result.

Proposition 3.9
.

Let
A,\widetilde{A}\in\{B,F\}
. Let
p_{i}\in(0,\infty]\,(p_{0}\in(0,\infty)\text{ if }A=F\text{ and }p_{1}\in(0,\infty)\text{ if }\widetilde{A}=F),q_{i}\in(0,\infty]
and
s_{i}\in\mathbb{R},
where
i=0,1
. Assume that

A^{s_{0}}_{p_{0},q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow\widetilde{A}^{s_{1}}_{p_{1},q_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Then, for
r\in(0,\infty]
and
b\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

T^{b}_{r}A^{s_{0}}_{p_{0},q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow T^{b}_{r}\widetilde{A}^{s_{1}}_{p_{1},q_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Proof.

Apply (
3.1
).
∎

Proposition 3.10
.

(i)

Let
A\in\{B,F\}
. Let
p\in(0,\infty]\,(p\in(0,\infty)\text{ if }A=F),q,r\in(0,\infty],s\in\mathbb{R}
and
b>0
. Then

(3.14)

T^{b}_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow T^{-b}_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

and

(3.15)

\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{T}^{-b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(ii)

Let
A,\widetilde{A}\in\{B,F\}
. Let
p\in(0,\infty]\,(p\in(0,\infty)\text{ if }A=F\text{ or }\widetilde{A}=F),q_{0},q_{1},r\in(0,\infty],s,b\in\mathbb{R}
and
\varepsilon>0
. Then

(3.16)

T^{b}_{r}A^{s+\varepsilon}_{p,q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow\widetilde{A}^{s}_{p,q_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow T^{b}_{r}A^{s-\varepsilon}_{p,q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

(3.17)

\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s+\varepsilon}_{p,q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow A^{s}_{p,q_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s-\varepsilon}_{p,q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(iii)

Let
p\in(0,\infty),q,r\in(0,\infty]
and
s,b\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

(3.18)

T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,\min\{p,q\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow T^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,\max\{p,q\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

In particular

T^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(iv)

Let
p\in(0,\infty),q,r\in(0,\infty]
and
s,b\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

(3.19)

T^{b}_{\min\{p,r\}}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow T^{b}_{\max\{p,r\}}F^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

In particular

T^{b}_{p}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{p}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(v)

Let
p\in(0,\infty)
and
s,b\in\mathbb{R}
. Then

T^{b}_{p}B^{s}_{p,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=T^{b}_{p}F^{s}_{p,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{p}F^{s}_{p,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Before we give the proof of this proposition, we observe that the new scale of spaces
T^{b}_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
enables to sharpen classical embeddings. For instance, let us illustrate this phenomenon with the well-known embeddings

(3.20)

B^{s}_{p,\min\{p,q\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow B^{s}_{p,\max\{p,q\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

where
p\in(0,\infty),q\in(0,\infty]
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
,
and their special case (taking
q=2
), for
1<p<\infty
and
s\in\mathbb{R}
,

(3.21)

B^{s}_{p,\min\{p,2\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow H^{s}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow B^{s}_{p,\max\{p,2\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Corollary 3.11
.

(i)

Let
s\in\mathbb{R},p\in(0,\infty)
and
q\in(0,\infty]
. Then

\displaystyle B^{s}_{p,\min\{p,q\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow T_{\min\{p,q\}}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

(3.22)

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow T_{\max\{p,q\}}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow B^{s}_{p,\max\{p,q\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

(ii)

Let
s\in\mathbb{R}
and
p\in(1,\infty)
. Then

\displaystyle B^{s}_{p,\min\{p,2\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow T_{\min\{p,2\}}F^{s}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\hookrightarrow H^{s}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow T_{\max\{p,2\}}F^{s}_{p,2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow B^{s}_{p,\max\{p,2\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Proof.

(i): Since
F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=\mathfrak{T}_{q}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=T_{q}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(cf. Proposition
3.8
), it follows from (
3.18
)-(
3.19
) that

F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow T_{\max\{p,q\}}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow T_{\max\{p,q\}}B^{s}_{p,\max\{p,q\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=B^{s}_{p,\max\{p,q\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

and

F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookleftarrow T_{\min\{p,q\}}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookleftarrow T_{\min\{p,q\}}B^{s}_{p,\min\{p,q\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=B^{s}_{p,\min\{p,q\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Specializing (i) with
q=2
and
p\in(1,\infty)
one gets (ii).
∎

Remark 3.12
.

Embeddings given in Corollary
3.11
are non trivial improvements of the classical ones (
3.20
) and (
3.21
). For instance, assume
p>q
then by (
3.22
),

F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow T_{p}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow B^{s}_{p,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Furthermore these embeddings are strict. Indeed, consider the lacunary Fourier series
f
defined by (
3.9
). Since (
3.10
) holds, we have

\|f\|_{F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\asymp\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jsq}|a_{j}|^{q}\bigg)^{1/q},

\|f\|_{T_{p}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\asymp\bigg(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\bigg(\sum_{j=2^{k}-1}^{2^{k+1}-2}2^{jsq}|a_{j}|^{q}\bigg)^{p/q}\bigg)^{1/p},

and

\|f\|_{B^{s}_{p,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\asymp\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jsp}|a_{j}|^{p}\bigg)^{1/p}.

Taking
a_{j}=2^{-js}(j+1)^{-1/q}(1+\log(1+j))^{-\varepsilon}
where
1/p<\varepsilon<1/q
, we have

\|f\|_{F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\asymp\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(1+\log j)^{-\varepsilon q}\frac{1}{j}=\infty

and

\|f\|_{T_{p}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}\asymp\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}k^{-\varepsilon p}<\infty.

On the other hand, if
a_{j}=2^{-js}(1+j)^{-\varepsilon}
with
1/p<\varepsilon<1/q
then

\|f\|_{B^{s}_{p,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}\asymp\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}j^{-\varepsilon p}<\infty

and

\|f\|_{T_{p}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}\asymp\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\bigg(\sum_{j=2^{k}}^{2^{k+1}}j^{-\varepsilon q}\bigg)^{p/q}\asymp\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}2^{-kp(\varepsilon-1/q)}=\infty.

The above argument also shows that the embeddings

B^{s}_{p,p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subsetneqq T_{p}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\subsetneqq F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\qquad p<q

are strict.

Proof of Proposition
3.10
.

(i): We start by proving the right-hand side embedding with
A\in\{B,F\}
. It is clear that

\|f\|_{T^{-b}_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-jbr}\bigg)^{1/r}\|f\|_{A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

where the last sum is convergent since
b>0
. We also have

\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-jbr}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}2^{\nu sq}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}(x)|^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\bigg)^{1/r}\lesssim\bigg(\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}2^{\nu sq}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}(x)|^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}

for
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and thus
\|f\|_{\mathfrak{T}^{-b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\lesssim\|f\|_{F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Concerning the left-hand side embedding of (
3.14
) with
A=B
, we have

\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}2^{\nu sq}\|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\leq 2^{-jbq}\,\|f\|^{q}_{T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\qquad j\geq 0,

for
f\in T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Summing up the previous estimates, we obtain

\displaystyle\|f\|^{q}_{B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}2^{\nu sq}\|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}

\displaystyle\leq\|f\|^{q}_{T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-jbq}\asymp\|f\|^{q}_{T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Similarly, to deal with
A=F
in (
3.14
), we note that

(3.23)

\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}2^{\nu sq}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 2^{-jb}\,\|f\|_{T^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\qquad j\geq 0,

for
f\in T^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Furthermore, we claim that

(3.24)

\|f\|_{F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}2^{\nu sq}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\min\{p,q\}}\bigg)^{1/\min\{p,q\}}.

Assuming momentarily that (
3.24
) is true, then the embedding
T^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
follows directly from (
3.23
).

To prove (
3.24
), we shall consider two possible cases. Firstly, if
p\geq q
we can estimate
\|f\|_{F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}
using the Minkowski’s inequality, specifically,

\displaystyle\|f\|_{F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}2^{\nu sq}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}2^{\nu sq}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}.

Secondly, if
p<q
then we make use of the fact that
\ell_{p}\hookrightarrow\ell_{q}
and apply Fubini’s theorem in order to get

\|f\|_{F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}2^{\nu sq}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}\bigg)^{1/p}.

Hence (
3.24
) holds.

The proof of the left-hand side embedding in (
3.15
), i.e.,
\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
follows also from (
3.24
) since (cf. (
3.23
))

\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}2^{\nu sq}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\leq 2^{-jb}\,\|f\|_{\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},\qquad j\geq 0,

for
f\in\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
.

(ii): Concerning the left-hand side embedding in (
3.16
), in light of (
3.20
) and Proposition
3.9
, it is enough to prove that

T^{b}_{r}B^{s+\varepsilon}_{p,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow B^{s}_{p,\xi}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

for some
\xi>0
sufficiently small. Applying Hölder’s inequality, we get

\displaystyle\|f\|_{B^{s}_{p,\xi}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}(2^{\nu s}\|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})})^{\xi}\bigg)^{1/\xi}

\displaystyle\lesssim\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-2^{j}\varepsilon\xi}\sup_{\nu=2^{j}-1,\ldots,2^{j+1}-2}\,\{2^{\nu(s+\varepsilon)}\|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\}^{\xi}\bigg)^{1/\xi}

\displaystyle\lesssim\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-2^{j}\varepsilon\xi}2^{-jb\xi}\bigg)^{1/\xi}\,\|f\|_{T^{b}_{\infty}B^{s+\varepsilon}_{p,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|f\|_{T^{b}_{r}B^{s+\varepsilon}_{p,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

On the other hand, in a similar fashion as above, the right-hand side embedding in (
3.16
) would follow from the special case

B^{s}_{p,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow T^{b}_{r}B^{s-\varepsilon}_{p,\xi}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\qquad\xi>0.

To prove the latter embedding, one can reason as follows:

\displaystyle\|f\|_{T^{b}_{r}B^{s-\varepsilon}_{p,\xi}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jbr}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}2^{\nu(s-\varepsilon)\xi}\|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\xi}\bigg)^{r/\xi}\right)^{1/r}

\displaystyle\lesssim\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-2^{j}\varepsilon r}2^{jbr}\bigg)^{1/r}\,\|f\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\asymp\|f\|_{B^{s}_{p,\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Next we turn our attention to the left-hand side embedding in (
3.17
). Note that it is enough to show that

(3.25)

\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s+\varepsilon}_{p,q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow B^{s}_{p,\xi}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

for some
\xi>0
sufficiently small; cf. (
3.20
). Let
j\in\mathbb{N}_{0}
and
\nu\in\{2^{j}-1,\ldots,2^{j+1}-2\}
. Since

\displaystyle\|2^{\nu s}(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-2^{j}\varepsilon}\,\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}2^{\nu(s+\varepsilon)q_{0}}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q_{0}}\bigg)^{1/q_{0}}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\lesssim 2^{-2^{j}\varepsilon}\,2^{-jb}\,\|f\|_{\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s+\varepsilon}_{p,q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},

we have

\displaystyle\|f\|_{B^{s}_{p,\xi}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}\|2^{\nu s}(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{\xi}\bigg)^{1/\xi}

\displaystyle\lesssim\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{-2^{j}\varepsilon\xi}\,2^{j(1-b\xi)}\bigg)^{1/\xi}\,\|f\|_{\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s+\varepsilon}_{p,q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\asymp\|f\|_{\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s+\varepsilon}_{p,q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

This completes the proof of (
3.25
). Furthermore, similar ideas as above can be applied to show the right-hand side embedding in (
3.17
). This is left to the interested reader.

(iii): It follows immediately from Proposition
3.9
and (
3.20
).

(iv): We only provide the proof of the left-hand side embedding of (
3.19
). If
p\geq r
then we can apply Minkowski’s inequality so that

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jbr}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}2^{\nu sq}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\bigg)^{1/r}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jbr}\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}2^{\nu sq}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{r}\bigg)^{1/r}=\|f\|_{T^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

On the other hand, if
r\geq p
then

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jbp}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}2^{\nu sq}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q}\bigg)^{p/q}\bigg)^{1/p}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle=\bigg(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jbp}\bigg\|\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}2^{\nu sq}|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}|^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}\bigg\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}^{p}\bigg)^{1/p}=\|f\|_{T^{b}_{p}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Item (v) is an immediate consequences of Fubini’s theorem.

∎

Remark 3.13
.

A detailed study of embedding theorems between
T^{b}_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
-spaces will be postponed until Sections
10
–
13
. In particular, the optimality of (
3.18
) will be addressed in Corollary
11.3
below, namely,

T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,u}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow T^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\iff u\leq\min\{p,q\}

and

T^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,u}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\iff u\geq\max\{p,q\}.

4.
Characterizations by interpolation

4.1.
Limiting interpolation. General facts.

The goal of this section is to show that the spaces
T^{b}_{r}A^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),\\
A\in\{B,F\}
, and
\mathfrak{T}^{b}_{r}F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
can be generated as interpolation spaces of the classical spaces
B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
F^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(cf. Theorems
4.2
,
4.8
and
4.20
below). As a consequence, we shall conclude not only that these new scales are closed under interpolation (cf. Theorems
4.7
and
4.14
below), but also new interpolation formulae involving
\mathbf{B}^{0,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and
\text{Lip}^{s,b}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(cf. Corollary
4.19
). To get all these results we shall apply the machinery provided by limiting interpolation. Next we briefly recall the construction of this method.

Let
(A_{0},A_{1})
be a couple of quasi-Banach spaces. Given
t>0
and
f\in A_{0}+A_{1}
, the
Peetre’s
K
-functional
is
defined by

(4.1)

K(t,f):=K(t,f;A_{0},A_{1}):=\inf\{\|f_{0}\|_{A_{0}}+t\|f_{1}\|_{A_{1}}:f=f_{0}+f_{1},\,f_{j}\in A_{j}\}.

For
\theta\in(0,1),\,q\in(0,\infty]
and
b\in\mathbb{R}
, the interpolation space
(A_{0},A_{1})_{\theta,q,b}
is formed by all those
f\in A_{0}+A_{1}
such that

(4.2)

\|f\|_{(A_{0},A_{1})_{\theta,q,b}}:=\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}(t^{-\theta}(1+|\log t|)^{b}K(t,f))^{q}\frac{dt}{t}\bigg)^{1/q}<\infty

(with the usual modification if
q=\infty
). See
[
Gu78
]
,
[
DRS79
]
and
[
GOT05
]
. In the special case
b=0
one recovers the classical interpolation space
(A_{0},A_{1})_{\theta,q}
, i.e.,

(4.3)

\|f\|_{(A_{0},A_{1})_{\theta,q}}:=\bigg(\int_{0}^{\infty}(t^{-\theta}K(t,f))^{q}\frac{dt}{t}\bigg)^{1/q}

cf.
[
BL76
]
,
[
BS88
]
and
[
Tri78
]
. Since
K(t,f;A_{0},A_{1})=tK(t^{-1},f;A_{1},A_{0})
, one has

(4.4)

(A_{0},A_{1})_{\theta,q,b}=(A_{1},A_{0})_{1-\theta,q,b}

with equality of quasi-norms.

The introduction of limiting interpolation spaces requires some non-trivial modifications of the quasi-norms (
4.2
) and (
4.3
). Indeed, note that setting
\theta=0
or
\theta=1
we only obtain in general the trivial space
\{0\}
. To overcome this issue we make the elementary observation that, working with ordered couples (i.e.,
A_{1}\hookrightarrow A_{0}
),

(4.5)

\|f\|_{(A_{0},A_{1})_{\theta,q}}\asymp\bigg(\int_{0}^{1}(t^{-\theta}K(t,f))^{q}\frac{dt}{t}\bigg)^{1/q},\qquad\theta\in(0,1).

This follows from the fact
K(t,f)\asymp\|f\|_{A_{0}}
for
t>1
. Accordingly, one may consider the integral given in the right-hand side of (
4.5
) to introduce the space
(A_{0},A_{1})_{\theta,q}
. This together with the additional refinement given by logarithmic weights allow us to define limiting interpolation spaces. More precisely, given an ordered couple of quasi-Banach spaces
(A_{0},A_{1})
, for
\theta\in\{0,1\},q\in(0,\infty]
and
b\in\mathbb{R}
, the
limiting interpolation space

(A_{0},A_{1})_{(\theta,b),q}
is the set of all those
f\in A_{0}
for which

(4.6)

\|f\|_{(A_{0},A_{1})_{(\theta,b),q}}=\bigg(\int_{0}^{1}(t^{-\theta}(1-\log t)^{b}K(t,f))^{q}\frac{dt}{t}\bigg)^{1/q}

is finite (with the usual modification if
q=\infty
). See
[
JM91
,
GOT05
,
CD15a
,
ALM19
]
and the references given there. It is not hard to check that if
b<-1/q\,(b\leq 0\text{ if }q=\infty)
then
(A_{0},A_{1})_{(0,b),q}=A_{0}
and if
b\geq-1/q\,(b>0\text{ if }q=\infty)
then
(A_{0},A_{1})_{(1,b),q}=\{0\}
. Accordingly, we may assume without loss of generality that
b\geq-1/q\,(b>0\text{ if }q=\infty)
if
\theta=0
and
b<-1/q\,(b\leq 0\text{ if }q=\infty)
if
\theta=1
. Under these assumptions, the
((\theta,b),q)
-method produces intermediate spaces between
A_{0}
and
A_{1}
, that is,

(4.7)

A_{1}\hookrightarrow(A_{0},A_{1})_{(\theta,b),q}\hookrightarrow A_{0},

and it satisfies the interpolation property for bounded linear operators. Furthermore, the left-hand side embedding in (
4.7
) is dense whenever
q<\infty
. We remark that it is possible to introduce limiting interpolation spaces for general couples of quasi-Banach spaces (not necessarily ordered) with the help of adequate slowly varying functions, however for the purposes of this paper is enough to restrict ourselves to the ordered case.

The following relations between classical and limiting interpolation will be useful; cf.
[
EOP02
, Corollaries 7.8 and 7.11]
and
[
DT18
, Lemma 2.2]
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\theta\in(0,1),\,q,r,r_{0},r_{1}\in(0,\infty]
and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}
.

(i)

If
b\geq-1/r
then

(A_{0},(A_{0},A_{1})_{\theta,q,\xi})_{(0,b),r}=(A_{0},A_{1})_{(0,b),r}.

(ii)

If
b<-1/r
then

((A_{0},A_{1})_{\theta,q,\xi},A_{1})_{(1,b),r}=(A_{0},A_{1})_{(1,b),r}.

(iii)

If
0<b_{0}+1/r_{0}<b_{1}+1/r_{1}
then

((A_{0},A_{1})_{(0,b_{0}),r_{0}},(A_{0},A_{1})_{(0,b_{1}),r_{1}})_{\theta,q}=(A_{0},A_{1})_{(0,\gamma),q}

where
\gamma=(1-\theta)(b_{0}+1/r_{0})+\theta(b_{1}+1/r_{1})-1/q
.

(iv)

If
b_{1}+1/r_{1}<b_{0}+1/r_{0}<0
then

((A_{0},A_{1})_{(1,b_{0}),r_{0}},(A_{0},A_{1})_{(1,b_{1}),r_{1}})_{\theta,q}=(A_{0},A_{1})_{(1,\gamma),q}

where
\gamma=(1-\theta)(b_{0}+1/r_{0})+\theta(b_{1}+1/r_{1})-1/q
.

(v)

If
b>-1/r
then

((A_{0},A_{1})_{(0,b),r},A_{1})_{\theta,q}=(A_{0},A_{1})_{\theta,q,(1-\theta)(b+1/r)}.

(vi)

If
b<-1/r
then

(A_{0},(A_{0},A_{1})_{(1,b),r})_{\theta,q}=(A_{0},A_{1})_{\theta,q,\theta(b+1/r)}.

4.2.
T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
spaces via interpolation

Theorem 4.2

(Characterization of
T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
via limiting interpolation)
.

Let
A\in\{B,F\}
. Let
p,q,q_{0},r\in(0,\infty]\,(p<\infty\text{ if }A=F),s,s_{0}\in\mathbb{R},s\neq s_{0}
, and
b\in\mathbb{R},\,b\neq 0
.

(i)

If
s_{0}>s
and
b>0
then

(4.8)

T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=(B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),A^{s_{0}}_{p,q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{(0,b-1/r),r}.

(ii)

If
s_{0}<s
and
b<0
then

(4.9)

T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=(A^{s_{0}}_{p,q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{(1,b-1/r),r}.

Remark 4.3
.

Note that the assumption
s_{0}>s
in (i) implies that
A^{s_{0}}_{p,q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(cf.
[
Tri83
, Section 2.3.2, page 47]
). A similar comment applies to (ii).

Remark 4.4
.

The case
b=0
in Theorem
4.2
is more delicate since the outcome is not the expected spaces
T_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
but
T^{*}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
(cf. (
3.5
)). This will be treated in detail in Remark
4.5
below.

Proof of Theorem
4.2
.

The proof relies on the retraction method (see

[
Tri78
, Section 1.2.4]
for details on this method) and limiting interpolation techniques. Assume first
p\in[1,\infty]
and
A=B
. In this case, it is well known that

B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a retract of
\ell^{s}_{q}(L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
and

B^{s_{0}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a retract of

\ell^{s_{0}}_{q}(L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
with the corresponding co-retraction operator being

(4.10)

\mathfrak{J}f:=((\varphi_{j}\widehat{f})^{\vee})_{j=0}^{\infty}.

Furthermore, elementary computations lead to

(4.11)

K(t,x;\ell^{s}_{q}(L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\ell^{s_{0}}_{q}(L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))\asymp\bigg(\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}[\min(2^{\nu s},t2^{\nu s_{0}})\|x_{\nu}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}]^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}

for
t>0
and
x=(x_{\nu})_{\nu=0}^{\infty}\in\ell^{s}_{q}(L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))+\ell^{s_{0}}_{q}(L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

(i): Assume
s_{0}>s
. According to (
4.11
), for every
j\geq 0
,

\displaystyle K(2^{-j(s_{0}-s)},x;\ell^{s}_{q}(L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\ell^{s_{0}}_{q}(L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))

(4.12)

\displaystyle\hskip-142.26378pt\asymp 2^{-j(s_{0}-s)}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=0}^{j}[2^{\nu s_{0}}\|x_{\nu}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}]^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}+\bigg(\sum_{\nu=j+1}^{\infty}[2^{\nu s}\|x_{\nu}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}]^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\|x\|_{(\ell^{s}_{q}(L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\ell^{s_{0}}_{q}(L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))_{(0,b-1/r),r}}

\displaystyle\hskip-113.81102pt\asymp\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}[(1+j)^{b-1/r}K(2^{-j(s_{0}-s)},x;\ell^{s}_{q}(L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\ell^{s_{0}}_{q}(L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))]^{r}\right)^{1/r}

(4.13)

\displaystyle\hskip-113.81102pt\asymp\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(1+j)^{(b-1/r)r}2^{-j(s_{0}-s)r}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=0}^{j}[2^{\nu s_{0}}\|x_{\nu}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}]^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\right)^{1/r}

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt+\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(1+j)^{(b-1/r)r}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=j}^{\infty}[2^{\nu s}\|x_{\nu}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}]^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\right)^{1/r}

\displaystyle\hskip-113.81102pt=:I+II.

Furthermore,

(4.14)

I\lesssim II.

Indeed, it follows from Hardy’s inequality (
B.3
) that

I\lesssim\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\left[(1+j)^{b-1/r}2^{js}\|x_{j}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}\right]^{r}\right)^{1/r}\leq II.

Combining (
4.13
) and (
4.14
), we obtain that

(4.15)

\|x\|_{(\ell^{s}_{q}(L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\ell^{s_{0}}_{q}(L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))_{(0,b-1/r),r}}\asymp\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(1+j)^{(b-1/r)r}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=j}^{\infty}[2^{\nu s}\|x_{\nu}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}]^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\right)^{1/r}.

In virtue of the retraction method and (
4.15
) we get

\displaystyle\|f\|_{(B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),B^{s_{0}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{(0,b-1/r),r}}

\displaystyle\asymp\|\mathfrak{J}f\|_{(\ell^{s}_{q}(L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\ell^{s_{0}}_{q}(L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))_{(0,b-1/r),r}}

(4.16)

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt\asymp\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(1+j)^{(b-1/r)r}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=j}^{\infty}[2^{\nu s}\|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}]^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\right)^{1/r}.

Assume further
b>0
. We claim

(4.17)

\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(1+j)^{(b-1/r)r}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=j}^{\infty}[2^{\nu s}\|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}]^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\right)^{1/r}\asymp\|f\|_{T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

Assuming momentarily the validity of the previous assertion, we conclude by (
4.16
) that (i) holds for
A=B,\,p\in[1,\infty]
and
q_{0}=q\in(0,\infty]
. Next we show (
4.17
). By monotonicity properties and Hardy’s inequality (note that
b>0
; cf. (
B.4
))

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(1+j)^{(b-1/r)r}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=j}^{\infty}[2^{\nu s}\|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}]^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\right)^{1/r}

\displaystyle\asymp

\displaystyle\hskip-170.71652pt\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jbr}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}}^{\infty}[2^{\nu s}\|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}]^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\right)^{1/r}

(4.18)

\displaystyle\hskip-170.71652pt\asymp\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{jbr}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=2^{j}-1}^{2^{j+1}-2}[2^{\nu s}\|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}]^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\right)^{1/r}=\|f\|_{T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}.

The non-diagonal case
q_{0}\neq q
can reduced to the previous case via the reiteration formula given in Lemma
4.1
(i). Indeed, let
s_{1}
be such that
s_{0}\in(s,s_{1})
. By the well-known interpolation properties of Besov spaces (cf.
[
BL76
, Theorem 6.4.5]
,
[
Tri78
, 2.4.1]
and
[
Tri83
, 2.4.2]
)

(4.19)

B^{s_{0}}_{p,q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=(B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),B^{s_{1}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{\theta,q_{0}}

where
\theta\in(0,1)
is given by
s_{0}=(1-\theta)s+\theta s_{1}
. Applying now Lemma
4.1
(i) we have

\displaystyle(B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),B^{s_{0}}_{p,q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{(0,b-1/r),r}

\displaystyle=(B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),(B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),B^{s_{1}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{\theta,q_{0}})_{(0,b-1/r),r}

\displaystyle=(B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),B^{s_{1}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{(0,b-1/r),r}=T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

This completes the proof of (i) in the case
A=B
and
p\in[1,\infty]
. Furthermore, the case
p\in(0,1)
can be done in a similar fashion but now replacing
L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by the Hardy space
h_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
so that the operator
\mathfrak{J}
(cf. (
4.10
)) is well defined and the retraction method can be also applied.

The formula (i) with
A=F
follows immediately from the case
A=B
and the well-known embeddings

(4.20)

B^{s_{0}}_{p,\min\{p,q_{0}\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow F^{s_{0}}_{p,q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\hookrightarrow B^{s_{0}}_{p,\max\{p,q_{0}\}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

(see, e.g.,
[
Tri83
, (9), page 47]
).

(ii): Assume
s_{0}<s
and
b<0
. According to (
4.11
), for every
j\geq 0
,

\displaystyle K(2^{-j(s-s_{0})},x;\ell^{s_{0}}_{q}(L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\ell^{s}_{q}(L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))

\displaystyle\hskip-142.26378pt\asymp 2^{-j(s-s_{0})}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=0}^{j}[2^{\nu s}\|x_{\nu}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}]^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}+\bigg(\sum_{\nu=j+1}^{\infty}[2^{\nu s_{0}}\|x_{\nu}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}]^{q}\bigg)^{1/q}.

Thus, by Hardy’s inequality (
B.4
), we have

\displaystyle\|x\|_{(\ell^{s_{0}}_{q}(L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\ell^{s}_{q}(L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))_{(1,b-1/r),r}}

\displaystyle\hskip-113.81102pt\asymp\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}[2^{j(s-s_{0})}(1+j)^{b-1/r}K(2^{-j(s-s_{0})},x;\ell^{s_{0}}_{q}(L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})),\ell^{s}_{q}(L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})))]^{r}\right)^{1/r}

\displaystyle\hskip-113.81102pt\asymp\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(1+j)^{(b-1/r)r}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=0}^{j}[2^{\nu s}\|x_{\nu}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}]^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\right)^{1/r}

\displaystyle\hskip-85.35826pt+\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}2^{j(s-s_{0})r}(1+j)^{(b-1/r)r}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=j}^{\infty}[2^{\nu s_{0}}\|x_{\nu}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}]^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\right)^{1/r}

\displaystyle\hskip-113.81102pt\asymp\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(1+j)^{(b-1/r)r}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=0}^{j}[2^{\nu s}\|x_{\nu}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}]^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\right)^{1/r}.

Invoking now the retraction method we establish that

\|f\|_{(B^{s_{0}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{(1,b-1/r),r}}\asymp\left(\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(1+j)^{(b-1/r)r}\bigg(\sum_{\nu=0}^{j}[2^{\nu s}\|(\varphi_{\nu}\widehat{f})^{\vee}\|_{L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}]^{q}\bigg)^{r/q}\right)^{1/r}

and thus a similar argument as in (
4.18
) now with
b<0
yields that

\|f\|_{(B^{s_{0}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{(1,b-1/r),r}}\asymp\|f\|_{T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}

or equivalently,

(4.21)

(B^{s_{0}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{(1,b-1/r),r}=T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

This covers (ii) with
A=B,p\in[1,\infty]
and
q_{0}=q\in(0,\infty]
.

Assume
q_{0}\in(0,\infty],\,q_{0}\neq q
. Given
s_{0}<s
we take
s_{1}
with
s_{1}<s_{0}
and define
\theta\in(0,1)
such that
s_{0}=(1-\theta)s_{1}+\theta s
. Since
B^{s_{0}}_{p,q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})=(B^{s_{1}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{\theta,q_{0}}
(cf. (
4.19
)) we can apply (
4.21
) and Lemma
4.1
(ii) to get

\displaystyle T^{b}_{r}B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle=(B^{s_{1}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{(1,b-1/r),r}

\displaystyle=((B^{s_{1}}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{\theta,q_{0}},B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{(1,b-1/r),r}

\displaystyle=(B^{s_{0}}_{p,q_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),B^{s}_{p,q}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))_{(1,b-1/r),r},

that is, (ii) holds with
A=B
and
p\in[1,\infty]
. Furthermore, the modification of this method obtained by replacing
L_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
by
h_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
enables us to cover the full range
p\in(0,\infty]
. Finally, the case
A=F
follows from the
B
-case via the embeddings (
4.20
).
∎

Remark 4.5
.
